CrowdStrike CEO: AI Cyberattack Wave, Hyperspeed Hacks, & The Patching Problem

All-In Podcast(1.2M)·53:28·154.4K views·2026-11-24
George KurtzCEO, CrowdStrike
TL;DRAI has collapsed the cybersecurity "pyramid of pain," giving any activist or e-criminal nation-state-level attack capabilities and driving a 300% year-over-year surge in discovered vulnerabilities.
As a result, organizations face a "break or breach" reality—rushing to patch decades of technical debt at machine speed (often causing major public outages) while defending against autonomous attack frameworks like RTX that can compromise a company in under three hours. - The...

TL;DR

AI has collapsed the cybersecurity "pyramid of pain," giving any activist or e-criminal nation-state-level attack capabilities and driving a 300% year-over-year surge in discovered vulnerabilities. As a result, organizations face a "break or breach" reality—rushing to patch decades of technical debt at machine speed (often causing major public outages) while defending against autonomous attack frameworks like RTX that can compromise a company in under three hours.

The Agentic Threat & The "Mythos Moment"

  • The Hugging Face Watershed Moment: OpenAI deployed thousands of autonomous software agents with minimal guardrails, resulting in an end-to-end autonomous attack. George Kurtz noted the real "aha moment" wasn't the attack itself, but that defenders lacked frontier-level AI capabilities to understand it and had to rely on Chinese models because US frontier models refused their defensive requests.
  • Collapsing the Pyramid of Pain: Historically, cyber adversaries ranged from low-sophistication activists at the base, to e-crime ($20B/year industry) in the middle, to nation-states at the apex. With AI agents—the new "agent state"—nation-state capabilities are democratized down to anyone.
  • Trusted Access & Obliterated Models: CrowdStrike participates in Anthropic’s Glasswing and OpenAI’s Daybreak trusted access programs. However, open-weight models (like GLM and Qwen) are only ~3 months behind frontier models, and bad actors are using "obliterated" models (e.g., Warlock, CyberStrike) with post-trained guardrails removed to achieve parity when paired with the right harness engineering.
  • RTX Autonomous Attack Framework: Built in China and briefly open-sourced before being taken down, RTX allows a human to set a target and let sub-agents handle recon, vulnerability discovery, exploit building, privilege escalation, and data theft—completely compromising a company in under 3 hours. Over 12,000 forks of RTX now exist, and CrowdStrike is actively responding to RTX-driven incidents (including hits on Korean banks where agents targeted 200 organizations).

The Vulnerability Surge & "Break or Breach"

  • Exponential CVE Growth: In 2026 alone, 17,000 CVEs (Common Vulnerabilities and Exposures) have been found—representing 25% of all CVEs ever discovered in history—with a 300% year-over-year increase (October to October) and volume doubling monthly.
  • Dormant Code & Vulnerability Chaining: Most newly surfaced flaws are dormant bugs in legacy code (e.g., a PHP vulnerability dating back to 2004). AI excels at "vulnerability chaining"—combining 3, 10, or 100 low-risk flaws into a single high-risk remote code execution exploit.
  • Break or Breach: The traditional 30-day Microsoft patch window has collapsed. Organizations must now choose between rushing patches at machine speed (risking cascading infrastructure failures) or leaving systems unpatched and getting breached.
  • Outages as Second-Order AI Effects: Recent high-profile outages (e.g., FAA, Microsoft Outlook/Teams, Chase, United, Apple) are largely driven by companies scrambling to patch and modernize 25–30 years of technical debt. Some CEOs are even considering taking global services offline temporarily just to safely apply PHP or Java patches.

Open-Weight, Local AI, & AI Sovereignty

  • Shift Away from Frontier Token Maxing: Fortune 500 companies are moving away from pure frontier-model usage toward AI sovereignty and cost minimization, running open-weight models locally or on private servers and routing only complex edge cases to frontier labs.
  • Hardware & Local Inference: Both Kurtz and Jason Calacanis highlighted the rise of local compute—such as Apple Mac Studios (512GB RAM) and Nvidia DGX Sparks—enabling knowledge workers to run powerful inference locally.
  • Open Source as Defense: Despite political pushback against Chinese open-source models, Kurtz and Calacanis agreed that open source provides transparency ("sunlight") that allows defenders to inspect code, track forks, and neutralize threats.

Identity, Human Factors, & The Future of Defense

  • Why Break In When You Can Log In: Attackers bypass Multi-Factor Authentication (MFA) by stealing session tokens (e.g., the 30-day "trust this computer" passkeys in Outlook/Gmail) and setting up hidden inbox rules to intercept wires and communications.
  • North Korean IT Worker Infiltration: CrowdStrike uncovered a widespread scheme where North Korean operatives interview for remote tech jobs (often with cameras off), have company laptops shipped to "laptop farms" (e.g., in Texas), and VPN in to work—and gain internal system access. CrowdStrike’s first 40 customer notifications were 100% accurate; one company even exposed an operative by asking them to say "Kim Jong-un is a fat pig" on a call.
  • Spy vs. Spy: Even if AI rewrote all global code to have zero vulnerabilities, breaches would still occur via identity and authentication flaws. The future of defense requires an automated red-blue AI loop (offensive AI continuously testing defensive AI), alongside strong relationships with law enforcement (e.g., local FBI offices) since corporations lack legal authority (Title 50) to hack back.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • Local AI Explosion: "We come back here a year from now... and we look at how much local AI is going to be used, it's going to be off the charts in my opinion." — George Kurtz
  • Desktop AI Sovereignty: Every person in an organization will eventually be able to have a $12,000–$20,000 local machine (e.g., Mac Studio) on their desktop capable of handling 90% of knowledge-worker tasks locally. — Jason Calacanis
  • Apple's Local AI Opportunity: Apple is positioned to "kill it" financially and strategically with high-margin local AI hardware like the Mac Studio. — George Kurtz & Jason Calacanis

Notable Quotes

"In the agent state world as the new apex predator, everyone is now on the same playing field in terms of capabilities up and down the stack... you're minting more adversaries and you're democratizing destruction." — George Kurtz
"We are at a point what we're seeing is what I call break or breach... we're now paying the bill for all the tech debt that's built up over the last, you know, 25, 30 years. The bill's come due now." — George Kurtz
"Even if there were zero vulnerabilities, we had perfect code everywhere, the entire world had perfect code, you'd still have breaches... Why break in when you can log in?" — George Kurtz

ETF Edge on how the Nvidia saga impacts leveraged funds

CNBC Television(3.4M)·23:02·4.4K views·2026-11-24
Jensen HuangCEO, Nvidia Will RhindFounder and CEO, GraniteShares Mike AkinsFounding Partner, ETF Action
TL;DRNvidia’s board unanimously reinstated CEO Jensen Huang, sparking a 30% stock surge and record-setting trading volumes across leveraged single-stock ETFs like GraniteShares' 2x long Nvidia fund (NVDL).
). While retail traders are driving explosive growth in leveraged AI and tech products, ETF Action’s Mike Akins warns that compounding decay and poor market timing have cost investors roughly $15 billion since 2022. - Nvidia’s Reversal: Following a tumultuous period where...

TL;DR

Nvidia’s board unanimously reinstated CEO Jensen Huang, sparking a 30% stock surge and record-setting trading volumes across leveraged single-stock ETFs like GraniteShares' 2x long Nvidia fund (NVDL). While retail traders are driving explosive growth in leveraged AI and tech products, ETF Action’s Mike Akins warns that compounding decay and poor market timing have cost investors roughly $15 billion since 2022.

The Nvidia Whiplash & Record ETF Volumes

  • Nvidia’s Reversal: Following a tumultuous period where Nvidia’s board stated founder and CEO Jensen Huang would not return, the board made a complete U-turn and unanimously reinstated him, triggering a 30% surge—price action not seen in Nvidia since the early 2000s.
  • Exploding Trading Volume:
    • Mike Akins noted that both the semiconductor levered ETF (SOXL) and NVDL were already trading well over 100% of their typical full-day volume mid-session.
    • Will Rhind reported that NVDL—the world's largest leveraged Nvidia ETF—was on pace to trade more than $2 billion on the day, potentially setting its highest-ever daily volume record.
  • Anthropic ETFs Overshadowed: GraniteShares launched the first 2x long (AIL) and 2x short (ANS) Anthropic ETFs the same day, which Rhind described as a "sideshow" compared to the Nvidia frenzy.
  • Market Health Concerns: Both Akins and Rhind cautioned that extreme whipsaw moves—large drawdowns followed by massive spikes—are historically unhealthy and typically signal recessionary or bear-market conditions, which Akins believes currently characterize the AI trade.

Mechanics, Retail Dominance, and Investor Timing

  • Daily Reset vs. Long-Term Compounding: Akins emphasized that leveraged single-stock ETFs are strictly single-day trading vehicles, not buy-and-hold investments.
    • Over the three years ending the prior Friday's close, Nvidia was up 150%, while 2x long NVDL was up only 100%—trailing the 300% return an investor might naively expect due to compounding effects.
  • Retail-Driven Ownership: According to 13F institutional reporting as of September 30, 97% of NVDL’s ownership is "unknown," indicating it is overwhelmingly a retail trading vehicle.
  • The $15 Billion Timing Gap: Since 2022, roughly $33 billion has flowed into single-stock leveraged ETFs, yet total assets sat at just $17 billion as of Friday's close—a ~$15 billion delta that Akins attributed to poor market timing by investors.

Fee Structures vs. Traditional Leverage

  • Competitive Packaging: Akins noted that while leveraged ETFs carry higher expense ratios than standard index funds, they offer retail investors at brokerages like Schwab or Fidelity a simpler, more liquid alternative to managing options or margin accounts, with no risk of margin calls.
  • Apples-to-Apples Cost Comparison: Rhind pushed back on fee criticism, arguing that leveraged ETFs should be compared to margin loans, options, or structured products rather than a Vanguard S&P 500 ETF.
    • Because the funds target 2x daily movement, the fee equates to less than 1 basis point per day (a fraction of 0.01%), making it "practically free" for daily traders compared to traditional leverage.

Industry Proliferation & Product Lifecycle

  • Rapid Sector Expansion: Akins highlighted massive growth in single-stock leveraged ETFs:
    • End of 2022: 22 funds totaling $900 million in AUM.
    • Prior to recent tech selloff: Eclipsed $45 billion in AUM.
    • As of Friday's close: 388 funds totaling $18 billion in AUM.
  • What Drives New Launches: Rhind explained that GraniteShares targets names with high investor enthusiasm, volume, and volatility—noting that low-volatility stocks like Berkshire Hathaway fail to gain traction in leveraged wrappers, while AI and crypto names thrive.
  • Reduced Taboo Around Closures: Rhind noted that leveraged ETFs only survive as long as underlying trader interest persists, and the industry is seeing more fund closures alongside back-to-back record launch years as issuers become more willing to shut down low-demand strategies.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • More Volatility Ahead: The recent whipsaw price action in Nvidia and the broader AI trade is forecasting continued volatility ahead. — *Mike Akins*
  • NVDL Volume Milestone: NVDL will probably trade more than $2 billion today, potentially marking its highest trading volume day ever. — *Will Rhind*
  • Sustained Bear Market Risk: Product issuers will continue launching leveraged funds where demand exists, but product proliferation and AUM will fall if the market enters a sustained bear market where "buying the dip" no longer works. — *Mike Akins*

Notable Quotes

"These types of whipsaw moves are not healthy. Historically speaking, we do not see these types of large drawdowns followed by huge drives up in a healthy market. It usually tends to be when you're in a recession or in a bear market territory, which we're not technically in for the broader market, but we are in for this AI trade." — *Mike Akins*
"We thought this was going to be the biggest news event of the day with Anthropic, the first leveraged ETFs going live. And I can tell you this is like a sideshow compared to what we're seeing with Nvidia." — *Will Rhind*
"Over the last kind of 2022 forward, there's been $33 billion that have come into single-stock levered ETFs, and they currently sit as of the close of Friday with $17 billion, so about a $15 billion difference. That delta is bad timing." — *Mike Akins*
"When you think about that in terms of fees, that's less than one basis point a day. It's a fraction of a basis point, a fraction of 100th of a percent. So for people, it's practically free if they're trading on a daily basis." — *Will Rhind*

How To Build A Jet Engine From Scratch

Y Combinator(2.4M)·15:53·112.2K views·2026-11-24
Jared FriedmanHost, YC Hard Tech Blake SchollFounder and CEO, Boom Supersonic
TL;DRAfter Rolls-Royce abruptly walked away from building the engine for Boom Supersonic’s 60-to-80-seat Overture airliner, Boom built its own engine core from scratch—and discovered it can first be deployed as a 42-megawatt natural-gas turbine for AI data centers, generating the cash flow and 1 million hours of operating data needed to fund and certify supersonic flight.
The Rolls-Royce Breakup: In September 2022, Rolls-Royce abruptly walked away from Boom’s engine partnership without warning, just as Boom had rolled out its XB-1 demonstrator and signed up United and American Airlines. Founder and CEO Blake Scholl described the breakup as...

TL;DR: After Rolls-Royce abruptly walked away from building the engine for Boom Supersonic’s 60-to-80-seat Overture airliner, Boom built its own engine core from scratch—and discovered it can first be deployed as a 42-megawatt natural-gas turbine for AI data centers, generating the cash flow and 1 million hours of operating data needed to fund and certify supersonic flight.

From Crisis to Dual-Use Engine

  • The Rolls-Royce Breakup: In September 2022, Rolls-Royce abruptly walked away from Boom’s engine partnership without warning, just as Boom had rolled out its XB-1 demonstrator and signed up United and American Airlines.
    • Founder and CEO Blake Scholl described the breakup as "the startup equivalent of a stage four cancer diagnosis," noting Boom could not raise a dollar or hold customer/supplier conversations overnight.
    • Boom launched a crash program in December 2022 to build its own engine, firing up its first full-scale engine core on November 21, 2026.
  • The AI Power Pivot (Superpower): In 2025, after XB-1 became the first independently developed jet to break the sound barrier, Scholl noticed 5-to-10-year grid interconnect queues and 5-year wait times for GE power turbines.
    • Scholl texted day-one Boom investor Sam Altman asking if a 40-megawatt-class natural gas turbine was relevant to AI; Altman replied: "yes, please. As many as you can make."
    • By removing the fan and adding a generator, Boom’s shared core converts from 40,000 lbs of thrust at Mach 1.7 (for Overture's Symphony engine) into 42 megawatts of clean electricity (for the Superpower turbine).
  • Funding & Certifying Overture: Selling turbines first allows Boom to self-fund Overture without raising billions from investors.
    • Making just 2 turbines a month creates a "very profitable multi-billion dollar business," and the day Boom ships its first unit, the company flips cash flow positive.
    • Jet engines are typically tested for ~5,000 hours before certification; running 24/7 in data centers will give Boom 1 million hours of real-world operating data to present to the FAA.

The Turbine Supply Bottleneck

  • Massive Unmet Demand: Host Jared Friedman (Y Combinator) notes the US needs ~100 GW of new generating capacity over the next 10 years—roughly $300 billion worth of Superpower turbines.
    • Boom’s original plan was to produce 1 GW of turbines per year, but a single prospective customer wants to deploy 1.6 GW every 30 days.
  • Incumbent Complacency: Scholl attributes the 5-year backlog (with GE gas turbines sold out through 2030) to $1–$2 trillion industrial incumbents founded over a century ago that refuse to invest in capacity because their prices and margins have tripled and Wall Street might punish capex.
  • Industrial Stagnation Since 1973: Scholl traces US industrial decay to 1973, the same year the US banned supersonic flight and halted the 6% annual compounded growth in electricity per capita that had held since 1882; since 2000, power production per capita has declined.

Vertical Integration at the Superpower Superfactory

  • Buying the Printer Over the Parts: When suppliers quoted Boom an 18-month lead time and $1.3 million for one ship set of 3D-printed turbine blades, Scholl discovered the printer itself cost $1.1 million with a 2-week lead time—so Boom bought the printer.
  • The South Denver Superfactory: Boom leased a nearly 200,000-square-foot facility in South Denver in January, building a vertically integrated factory from raw materials (stainless steel castings, nickel super alloy forgings) to completed turbines under one roof.
    • Co-locating machinists next to designers eliminates supply chain delays; during Sprint Core assembly, Boom’s machine shop fabricated a custom wrench overnight.
  • All-Digital Single-Crystal Foundry: Turbine blades must spin at 10,000 RPM at 3,000°F continuously for 3 years, requiring single-crystal blades (one perfect grid of atoms, like a diamond) currently made by only three companies worldwide.
    • Boom is building its own all-digital single-crystal foundry next door to design a blade on Monday, cast it in the afternoon, and test it on Tuesday.

Software-Driven Engineering & Small Teams

  • Every Hardware Team Has a Coder: Boom embeds a software engineer on every hardware team to automate workflows, and "every person at Boom down to the receptionist is a coder in the AI-enabled sense."
  • BladeRunner Software: Boom built a custom tool called BladeRunner with its own CAD kernel and GPU cluster.
    • One engineer can drag a slider and view real-time aerodynamics and structural behavior—compressing work that typically takes a team of 100 engineers 3 to 6 months into real time.
  • Small, Elite Headcount: Boom currently employs 165 people (~40 in manufacturing, ~125 in engineering and everything else), following the model of the 50-person team that built XB-1.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • First Turbine Shipments: Boom will begin shipping Superpower turbines in mid-2027 (*Jared Friedman*).
  • Cash Flow Positive: The day Boom ships its first Superpower turbine, the company will flip cash flow positive (*Blake Scholl*).
  • In-House Manufacturing: Boom expects to manufacture 85% of every Superpower turbine in-house by weight (*Blake Scholl*).
  • Headcount Cap: Outside of manufacturing, Boom will never exceed 250 employees (*Blake Scholl*).
  • Supersonic Odds: Boom has "better than 50% odds that we all get to fly supersonic on our airplane" (*Blake Scholl*).
  • US Power Demand: The US needs to add around 100 GW of new generating capacity in the next 10 years (*Jared Friedman*).

Notable Quotes

"The Rolls breakup was like the startup equivalent of a stage four cancer diagnosis. Meaning if we don't do something, this will definitely kill us." — *Blake Scholl*
"We put a bold thing out there internally, which is outside of manufacturing, Boom will never be larger than 250. We're going to do the power turbine and the supersonic airliner with a team of no more than 250." — *Blake Scholl*
"It doesn't have 5,000 hours. It has a million because it's been running 24/7 for years powering your tokens." — *Blake Scholl*
"There is nothing so human as flying because it is the thing that we do not have the biology to do, but we have the brains to do." — *Blake Scholl*

So, Boom is doing this thing that to me seems obvious, but is regarded as like a massive breakthrough that nobody thought of, which is we're putting every production process in one building under one roof, so we can go from raw materials to completed advanced products quickly without suppliers. That building is the nearly 200,000 square foot supersonic superactory in South Denver. So the the the vision for the superpower superactory is we go from raw materials to completed turbines under one roof with essentially every process vertically integrated. Building XB1, Blake learned it's the other way around. Vertical integration, bringing everything in house. Can you talk about like why that makes sense and why why Boom is so unusual in doing it this way? We believed wrongly in fact that we could not afford to vertically integrate. We thought we didn't have the capital to go buy the production equipment to stand at the in-house capability. so we thought we had to outsource. We thought we had to rent other people's equipment. This is actually the expensive and slow way to do it. We were lucky we survived the outsourcing. And Boom has learned that the best way to make parts in-house is to put the people who make them right next to the people who design them. When we were building Sprint Core, we found that like some parts were hard to reach and install. And we kind of wanted a wrench of a certain shape that is not a wrench that you can just buy. And so what did we do? One guy catted up a drawing for it. We sent it to our machine shop. The machine shop worked overnight and the next morning we had the exact perfect wrench for that assembly. And that is something that could happen like you know in a few hours that would be somewhere between weeks and just flat out impossible. The parts that go in this engine have to survive in extreme environments. So think spinning at 10,000 RPM at 3,000° F and doing that continuously for 3 years without breaking. That is the environment the turbine lives in. If and to do that requires the highest performance materials invented by mankind. The hardest of the hardware are single crystal and this is a this is a thing that according to the internet only three companies in the world can do and it's the sort of thing that if I describe to you you might think it was flat out impossible. Turbine blades the most advanced turbine blades are one crystal one perfect grid of atoms just like a diamond except they're much larger than a diamond. And and so there is a lot of industrial secret sauce locked up in kind of the big three engine makers. But how do you actually produce single crystal parts? Boom is building its own single crystal foundry here in the building next door. And our goal is not just to do single crystal, but to do the world's most advanced all digital single crystal. We're literally you can design a blade on Monday, cast it in the afternoon, and by Tuesday, you've got a cast a new cast part ready to test. Before Boom, Blake was a software engineer, and Boom builds its own software to run almost everything. And a thing we discovered 10 years ago was that most hardware engineering is actually software engineering. And so we did this thing where we put a software engineer on every hardware team and we said automate what your what your teammates are doing. Now the cost of software development has collapsed. Now every person at boom down to the receptionist is a coder in the AI enabled sense of that. The fastest anybody's ever done a whole engine iteration is 3 months. 6 months is more typical. We built a tool called Bladeunner where an engineer can drag a slider. We have our own CAD kernel. The geometry updates in real time. We have our own GPU cluster. This the aerodynamics simulation runs in real time. So one engineer looking at a screen can drag a slider and see how it impacts the aerodynamics and structural behavior of that blade. So one engineer in real time can do what used to take a team of 100 6 months and we're just getting started. Jeff Bezos On ‘AI For Atoms,’ Dealing With Democrats, & Picking The Next Bond | Compound Interest

Semafor(43.2K)·36:38·25.1K views·2026-11-24

Thesis & Technology: Unlike LLMs that manipulate symbols by reading the internet, engineering requires specialized models trained on physics and real-world constraints. Prometheus builds neural surrogates for numerical simulation using a single multiphysics model. Scale &...

TL;DR

Jeff Bezos and Prometheus co-founder Vik Bajaj unveiled details of their $14.5 billion "AI for atoms" startup—which is already compressing months-long engineering tasks into hours for early alpha partners like Blue Origin—while Bezos defended his outreach to President Trump, urged Nvidia’s board to hire internally, and revealed how Amazon Studios cast Robert Pattinson as the next James Bond.

Prometheus: "AI for Atoms" & The Manufacturing Economy

  • Thesis & Technology: Unlike LLMs that manipulate symbols by reading the internet, engineering requires specialized models trained on physics and real-world constraints. Prometheus builds neural surrogates for numerical simulation using a single multiphysics model.
    • Scale & Funding: Bezos invested at the seed stage in late 2024; the company now employs 250 people and has raised approximately $14.5 billion, with much of it committed to compute.
    • Early Benchmarks: Prometheus tools are designing analog chips and radios in 12 hours—work that typically takes a team of 10 to 20 people 6 to 9 months. A teammate from JPL also prompted the tools to redesign the Mars helicopter; the model explored quadcopters, counterrotating helicopters, and fixed-wing aircraft before calculating the blade chord for the Martian atmosphere.
  • Go-To-Market & Business Model:
    • Private Alpha: Currently in private alpha with early customers, including Blue Origin, which is using the tools on two active projects.
    • Open Ecosystem over Walled Garden: Vik Bajaj noted that the manufacturing market is worth tens of trillions of dollars, making a "walled garden" impossible. Prometheus will both make its tools broadly available and invest directly in some of the companies it helps, benefiting as a principal rather than exclusively as a toolmaker.
  • Data Trust & Supply Chain Impact:
    • Protecting IP: Addressing fears of frontier labs acting like "the fox in the hen house," Bajaj emphasized that Prometheus respects proprietary engineering traditions, trains on publicly observable product data, and generates much of its own synthetic data.
    • Accelerating Legacy Processes: Bezos cited precision aerospace casting—which still relies on trial-and-error X-rays and mold insulation—as an area where AI can create repeatable processes, potentially cutting 9-to-12-month part wait times down to a month.

Politics, Democrats, & Root-Cause Problem Solving

  • Engaging with Presidents: Facing questions from Liz Hoffman about whether his closeness to President Trump is a liability now that Democrats hold the gavel, Bezos stated he has worked with every president since Clinton (noting interactions with Biden were the most limited). He described Trump as uniquely communicative and accessible, but insisted his outreach has always been for the country's benefit rather than to advantage Amazon.
  • The "Pick a Villain" Trap: Bezos argued that while targeting a villain—historically lawyers, Wall Street, landlords, and now Big Tech—is effective politics, it fails to solve systemic problems.
    • Housing Affordability: Using housing as a core example, Bezos noted that high prices stem from subsidizing demand while constraining supply. He pointed to Austin, which lowered housing costs over the last 10 years despite significant population growth by increasing supply (such as eliminating requirements for two parking spots per apartment unit).
  • Tech & Democratic Priorities: Bajaj added that rapid technological advancement gives Democrats a rare opportunity to achieve long-standing policy goals in education, infrastructure, and healthcare at scale.

Nvidia, AI Investing, & Life Sciences

  • Advice for Nvidia's Board: Declining to weigh in on whether Jensen Huang should return due to a lack of inside boardroom details, Bezos nonetheless praised Huang's foresight. His advice to Nvidia's board: hire from within, as outside CEOs in complex companies have a "vanishingly small chance of succeeding." He dismissed comparisons to Amazon, noting his own departure was a multi-year, planned transition.
  • Investor Discernment: Bezos observed that early technology cycles fund every experiment ("the good, the bad, and the ugly"), but AI investors are now becoming more discerning and diverting capital to stronger ideas—a healthy sign for the market.
  • Healthcare & Biotech Timelines: Bajaj, who previously ran Google's life sciences business, stressed the need to "underpromise and overdeliver" in biology.
    • Early cancer detection has been a 10-year journey, though machine-learning blood tests are now on the market.
    • Drug discovery and development (a ~15-year effort) is on the verge of accelerating cures, driven by specialized AI firms like Isomorphic, Zera, and Initro.

Casting the Next James Bond

  • The Selection Process: Bezos confirmed he was heavily involved in Amazon Studios' search for the next 007. After narrowing a large pool to five finalists, the team ran traditional screen tests featuring full costumes, real sets, other actors, and three different scenes per finalist.
  • Why Robert Pattinson: Despite an initial slight bias against Pattinson due to his existing fame, Bezos said his screen tests made the choice "immediately undebated" alongside director Denis, leading to a final decision on Friday.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • "The entire manufacturing economy is going to be transformed by AI in the coming years." — Jeff Bezos
  • "Investors are going to get more discerning... [and] divert their resources to the better ideas. That's going to happen." — Jeff Bezos
  • "It won't be that we'll cure all disease in 10 years or something like that. You'll just see a[n] increase dramatically in the productivity of those things pretty quickly." — Vik Bajaj (on AI in drug discovery and healthcare)

Notable Quotes

"Pick a villain is a good political technique, but it's a terrible technique for solving problems... I don't look around at this dynamic economy and all the tools that people are building and all the productivity they're creating and say, 'What a bunch of villains.'" — Jeff Bezos
"All civilizational wealth comes from invention... What we're trying to do at Prometheus is accelerate that dream-build loop." — Jeff Bezos
"We have to be really calibrated about how long things take in the world of healthcare, in the world of living things, in the physical world, and we have to be scientifically rigorous and sort of underpromise and overdeliver." — Vik Bajaj

When you go to cast a part, it's just as one example, but again, it's very common, you make a mold, you get molten metal, you pour the metal into the mold and you let the metal cool and harden. That sounds very simple, and for non-precision things, it is pretty simple. But if you can't have any flaws or voids or cracks in that cast part, it gets complicated because the metal cools at different rates. In some places, the mold is very skinny and those places are going to cool fast. And in some places, the mold is thick and those places are going to cool more slowly. And because the metal contracts when it cools, you get little voids and hairline fractures. So the way that the casting process works today is you make a part as we've just discussed and then you X-ray that part or get some industrial X-ray machine. You find the little hairline fractures and then you go back and you add insulation to the mold to slow the cooling rate in certain areas so that you end up with a mold insulated properly so that the part cools at a uniform rate. That is, believe it or not, done mostly by trial and error, and it's actually not an easy thing to simulate. So there are good reasons why it's done. It's experienced people studying X-rays deciding where to put insulation on the mold and trying again. OpenAI’s Noam Brown: Why Game Theory Didn’t Lead to General Intelligence (Live at No Limit Finals)

Sequoia Capital(258.0K)·30:39·19.2K views·2026-11-24
Noam BrownResearcher, OpenAI
TL;DROpenAI researcher Noam Brown left computational game theory behind after discovering that human-data modeling—not mathematical rationality—is the key to general intelligence, leading him to help build o1’s test-time reasoning.
Today, AI exhibits "jagged intelligence" that puts us in a "centaur period" of human-AI collaboration, though rapid recursive self-improvement leads Brown to advise promising students to drop out and join AI labs immediately. Early Poker Foundations: Brown began studying AI for...

TL;DR

OpenAI researcher Noam Brown left computational game theory behind after discovering that human-data modeling—not mathematical rationality—is the key to general intelligence, leading him to help build o1’s test-time reasoning. Today, AI exhibits "jagged intelligence" that puts us in a "centaur period" of human-AI collaboration, though rapid recursive self-improvement leads Brown to advise promising students to drop out and join AI labs immediately.

From Poker to Diplomacy: The Crisis of Faith in Game Theory

  • Early Poker Foundations: Brown began studying AI for poker in grad school in 2012, winning the MIT Pokerbots competition (which offered $10,000 in prize money) in both 2014 and 2015 using Game Theory Optimal (GTO) self-play via Counterfactual Regret Minimization (CFR).
  • Tournament Strategy: In a 60-minute, multiplayer, non-instant-runoff format like Sequoia's No Limit tournament ($100,000 prize pool), Brown noted that while K-means clustering plus 30 minutes of self-play can yield a low-fidelity GTO bot, an exploitation-based approach that targets opponent weaknesses is likely superior.
  • The Limits of Rationality in Diplomacy: In 2019 (around GPT-2's release), Brown targeted Diplomacy because it required natural language negotiation and cooperation. He realized game theory fails here because it assumes all players are rational—illustrated by the Ultimatum Game, where offering $0.01 out of $100 is game-theory optimal, yet humans will reject it out of spite.
  • Modeling Human Irrationality: Winning Diplomacy bots had to be trained on massive amounts of human data to imitate human behavior and irrationality. Host Alfred Lin connected this to Daniel Kahneman’s *Thinking, Fast and Slow*, noting that behavioral economics shows consistent "bugs" in human brains.
  • The 2022 Pivot: Realizing game theory was not the path to general intelligence caused a "crisis of faith" for Brown in early 2022, prompting him to pivot away from his core expertise toward general-purpose reasoning and reinforcement learning (RL).

The Reasoning Breakthrough & OpenAI's o1

  • Challenging the Pre-Training Dogma: When Brown joined OpenAI in 2022, much of the AI field believed simply scaling up pre-training would solve everything. Brown pointed out that GPT-4 couldn't play Go, chess, or poker, and scaling pre-training alone to play Go confidently would require roughly 6 orders of magnitude more compute.
  • Thinking Before Acting: Unlike early 2012–2013 poker bots that acted instantly due to short tournament clocks, humans pause to think through tough decisions.
  • Scaling Test-Time Compute: The key unlock for OpenAI's o1 was applying RL to chain-of-thought reasoning. Training models to think longer before responding proved to be a general-purpose way to scale test-time compute across any domain, rather than relying solely on pre-training scale.

The "Centaur Period" and Jagged Intelligence

  • Evaluating New Models: Brown tests every new model by asking it to build a poker bot. While GPT-6 Astro can now make money playing online poker, it still cannot build a full-scale poker bot on its own (though it can build an excellent river solver in a single prompt).
  • Where Models Still Fail: Models 5.5 and 5.6 were the first to 10x–20x Brown's bot-building speed, but current models still lack long-term planning and organization. For example, they will waste hours trying to optimize the numerics of CFR, failing to realize CFR is robust to small numerical errors.
  • Jagged Intelligence & Prompting: Alfred Lin highlighted that AI intelligence is "jagged"—superhuman in some areas and weak in others. Humans add value by recognizing these gaps and breaking complex tasks into bite-sized problems the model can solve.
  • The Centaur Period: Brown argued that most professions, including AI research, are entering a "centaur period" where human-plus-AI teams outperform either working alone.

Recursive Self-Improvement (RSI) & Talent Strategy

  • A Historical Loop: Brown framed RSI not as a new concept, but as the story of all human technological progress—from telescopes to moving from punch cards to compilers, and now using LLMs to filter pre-training data quality (a practice already underway in 2022–2023).
  • What's Different Now: Today's RSI loop is distinct due to the generality of the technology and its speed. While bottlenecks remain in compute and the "long tail" of tasks models are bad at, progress has accelerated over the past 6 months.
  • Drop Out Now: Given the steep rate of progress, Brown’s most controversial advice is that promising students should drop out of school and join AI labs immediately. He noted he has hired several dropouts who skipped their senior year of college and achieved exceptional success.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • Steep AI Progress: The AI field will continue to see a "very steep rate of progress over the next year or two" — *Noam Brown*
  • General-Purpose Game Evaluation: While LLMs cannot yet play real-time strategy games or *Zelda: Wind Waker* at a superhuman level, "Give it a year, who knows?" — *Noam Brown*
  • World Series of Poker (WSOP): Within the next 5 years, a single AI agent has a 20% chance of winning the WSOP against humans—not via GTO (due to high variance), but only if it becomes superhuman at reading human faces and detecting bluffs — *Noam Brown*

Notable Quotes

"The problem with game theory is that it assumes... that all the other players are rational and that it is common knowledge that all players are rational. And people are not actually rational." — *Noam Brown*
"I think we're entering a period, the centaur period, where the combination of human plus AI is going to be like most effective." — *Noam Brown*
"If somebody is a promising student, the best thing to do is actually to drop out and join the labs now." — *Noam Brown*
Please join me in a round of applause for Noam and Alfred. Hey Noam, thank you for coming. It's a rainy day and this is actually all because of you because you started studying poker for a while and you and I talked a little bit about your research before you went to OpenAI. You went from studying poker to studying game theory and diplomacy to now reasoning. Tell us a little bit about your path. Why did you decide to study poker of all things? Yeah, so first of all it's great to be here. It's really exciting to see all these people working like able to do so much in an hour. I think it's really incredible. My journey, I started I finished undergrad and I wasn't really sure what I wanted to do and I figured I wanted to do research and I wasn't really sure in what and when I was looking at grad schools there was this professor that was looking for people to work on AI to play poker and I thought this is great. I have a lot of interest in game theory. I have a lot of interest in computer science, economics and I had played poker a lot in high school and college and I loved the game and I thought it was always really fascinating that there was this perfect strategy to poker that nobody knew what it was, but if you could find it you could just make unlimited money. So I started working on that basically as soon as I started grad school in 2012 and worked on it continuously through my whole PhD and it's actually pretty unusual to work on something. Money during that time too? I never used it to play online poker, but there is a fun fact. The precursor to this I guess is the MIT Pokerbots competition which ran for several years and as a poor grad student I wanted to enter this competition because there was $10,000 in prize money and I wasn't at MIT so I got one of my MIT friends to collaborate with me and we submitted a poker bot. We won two years in a row, 2014 and 2015. And after that, I kind of felt bad about, you know, beating up on these people that don't really know how to make poker bots. And so we would have the agents running, but we basically have the model training playing self through self play using CFR. Later on it turned into these more neural net based self play approaches, but everything was focused on self play to converge to GTO. And the problem there is that it takes a long time to train. The coding part you can do without modern LLMs. I think you could make the code to run that in an hour, but the training process itself could take many hours. I think you could make a pretty low fidelity GTO bot in an hour. I think if you did K-means clustering to reduce the abstraction size to like a pretty small size and then did self play for like let's say 30 minutes, you could actually probably get a half decent poker bot. That said, the format because it's multiplayer and because you're not doing like an instant runoff kind of tournament where you're always going to like eliminate the weakest competitors, but you're actually playing against all the competitors, I could see an exploitation based approach actually being the better one for this kind of tournament structure. So I think in this setup, I would probably go for an exploitation based approach and try to observe the weaknesses of the opponents and then exploit that especially in a multi-handed a multiplayer situation. You're going to have to submit your own code next year. We're going to have you compete, but you can't win. So I hope you make enough at OpenAI, hopefully. Because the prize money is only $100,000. Because we've been tracking the performance of like I've been tracking the performance of these different general purpose LLMs over time, and with Astro like as reasoning models have developed, they've actually gotten quite good. And Astro I would say is actually like able to make money playing online poker at this point. Yeah, so maybe backing up a step because you obviously went from poker to diplomacy to reasoning and now these LLMs can do a lot more than just play poker and do it extremely well. Take us through your journey of how you took your learnings from poker to diplomacy, game theory around diplomacy to then into reasoning. I think that's a really interesting path. Yeah, the original motivation for working on poker was that there had been a lot of progress on Go and on Chess, these perfect information games. And the problem is that if you only have a technique that works on a very narrow domain, then it's actually not that useful, right? Because you want something that's general purpose. And so the motivation for working on poker was that we can figure out new things about what techniques actually work in these other domains and eventually make this more general purpose AI that can, for example, play both Go and poker. And after we made the poker bots, I think we did actually learn quite a bit, but the truth is that they weren't that good for Chess or Go. And so I wanted to try something more ambitious, something that would expand the scope of games that these techniques would be able to play. And at the time it was around 2019, GPT-2 had come out, and it started to look like there would be rapid progress in language models. And I thought like the original thing that I was discussing with my teammates was we want to make an AI that could play both go and poker. But considering how rapid progress in AI was in 2019, we thought that wouldn't be ambitious enough. And so we wanted to target the game that we thought would be the hardest to make an AI for, which we thought was diplomacy. Because it is a natural language game that involves negotiations with other players, because it's not just purely adversarial, it's also cooperative. You have to form alliances. In 2019 it was unthinkable that you could have an AI play this game well. And we thought that's the perfect thing to aim for. Poker took decades for people to work on. If we have to spend like five years making an AI that can finally play diplomacy, that's great. So we took it on and we learned a lot along the way. And one of the things that I took away is that actually the game theory approach doesn't actually work that well in diplomacy. I originally came in... Say more about that because I thought that game theory was developed to actually be a much better negotiator in a repeat game and especially in diplomacy. I think there is intuition that you can take away from game theory, but the problem with game theory is that it assumes this is the central premise in game theory, that it assumes that all the other players are rational and that it is common knowledge that all players are rational. And people are not actually rational. And if you come in with this assumption and you make it a central pillar that everybody's rational, then you're going to make mistakes. And I think a good example is, are you familiar with the ultimatum game? Well, you said it's playing... So it's this idea that there's two players, Alice and Bob. Alice has $100 and can offer Bob anywhere between $0 and $100. And Bob can either accept or reject. And if Bob rejects, they both get zero. If Bob accepts, then they split the money that way. So if Alice were to offer Bob $10 and Bob accepts, then Alice gets 90, Bob gets 10. And the game theory optimal approach for this game is to offer Bob like a penny. Yeah. Some small amount. Yeah, just a small amount. Yeah. And you know, like this makes sense. You know, Bob has a choice of making a penny or making zero. The penny's worth more. But I mean, you can imagine what would happen if you actually tried to offer somebody a penny in this game. Like the first thing they would... yeah, they would say no. So what we found is that our diplomacy bots when we took this very game theoretic approach would start doing these kinds of behaviors. And there's this temptation to say, oh, we can try to find, we can try to encode some semblance, some notion of vengeance or fairness into the bots. And when you go down this rabbit hole, eventually what we realized is that actually it's just too nuanced. And the real way to make a superhuman diplomacy AI is to train on human data, to just understand people to such an extent, to not take a game theoretic approach, to not assume rationality, but to just assume that people are going to behave like people, understand what that means by training on a lot of human data. And then determine what is my best course of action given the behaviors of everybody else. And so do you end up with a model of human irrationality then? That's the idea, that you end up with a model of human irrationality. So we train these models that their purpose is to imitate people as closely as possible. And then we train the AI with these imitation humans and do a lot of training and then we can deploy that in the real world with actual humans and that's what does best. There's a book by two behavioral economists about thinking fast and slow. And a lot of that has to do with Kahneman just explaining like there's kind of like consistent bugs in our brains and we sort of have these solutions that are imperfect. And you can explain why it happens from a behavioral economics point of view, but it isn't the rational reasoned approach. How have you incorporated that into reasoning and doing research in reasoning at OpenAI? Yeah, so after the diplomacy results, I had an interesting choice in my career at that point because this was around 2022. Around early 2022 it was becoming clear that the game theory approach was not working in diplomacy and actually the right approach was this kind of more modeling of human behavior. And this was kind of like a crisis of faith moment for me because my entire career up until that point had been about game theory, right? My area was computational game theory, my expertise was computational game theory. And I had to wrestle with the fact that actually that was not the path to general intelligence, that actually if you look at the evidence, it was this training on a lot of human data, modeling people really well, and then teaching the model to interact with that environment, and then also the scaling up of reasoning thing. And so I had to choose between, okay, well, do I stick with computational game theory despite me thinking that this is not the most important direction, the most promising path to general intelligence, or do I pivot and work on what I think is actually the most valuable thing despite it not being what I'm an expert at, which is general purpose reasoning in RL? And I ultimately decided to go the path of what I thought was most impactful, which was actually just pivot and work on this general reasoning direction. And so this led me to join OpenAI. I looked at a lot of different places and I told them what I want to work on. These LLMs, they're really cool, they're really general, but they can't play Go, they can't play poker. Why not? And at the time it was a very... it's hard to put yourself back in 2022. I guess people remember what it was like back then, but people were extremely bullish on just scaling up pre-training. And I would go to some places and I would say, look, reasoning is going to be this essential ingredient, and people would tell me, well, actually we should just pre-train a bigger model and that'll solve all the problems. And GPT-4 can't play Go, it sucks at chess, it sucks at poker, but if we just do a few more orders of magnitude of training, maybe it'll just work. And I would point to the evidence and say actually no, if you look at how much you would have to scale it up by for a game like Go to be able to play Go confidently, it would be about six orders of magnitude. And that doesn't seem realistic. Fortunately, OpenAI was actually really bullish about this direction. So and I didn't know this at the time, but they actually had a team working on this direction of how do you get general purpose reasoning out of these language models. And so ultimately I joined OpenAI and worked on what became o1 and now these models like they can play these games extremely well as just general purpose LLMs. What changed over the last few years? What was the breakthrough that allowed them to, as you said, it's not just scaling it up? So what were the major breakthroughs along the way? I think the major takeaway from my work on poker, diplomacy, and then also looking back at games like backgammon and chess was that you needed these AIs to think before acting. So when I started working on poker bots in 2012, in 2013, there was no reasoning going into these models. They would just act instantly. And there were a lot of reasons for this. A big factor was actually the structure of the tournament and this tournament included is that it doesn't really give you much time. It doesn't give the model much time to actually reason before it has to act. There's a very short clock and that's kind of essential because poker's a high variance game and so you need to play a lot of hands and so you can't have it sit there for 10 seconds thinking for every single hand. But people do that, right? People if they're faced with a tough decision will think through the decisions that they have to make. And that obviously benefits them instead of having to act instantly. So seeing this general trend of okay in all of these games, chess, Go, poker, diplomacy, we had to have these AIs think very carefully before acting in order to beat top humans. How do we get this out of... and at the time, there was chain of thought, which is the idea that you just prompt the model and say, think step by step before answering. And there was some evidence that this actually led to better solutions, but it was very coarse. It was not really sophisticated. And it turned out the key ingredient was, yeah, if you do reinforcement learning to train for this behavior, then you actually get much better chain of thought behavior, you get much better outputs. And then also what we observe at that point is actually the longer the model takes to think before responding, the better it does. And that was I think the key unlock that was like, okay, now we have a way to scale up test time compute not just in these games, but actually in a general purpose way with language models that can be applied to basically any domain. And they were really bad at first, but they're getting better and better. And I would say with 5.5, 5.6 was the first time that I felt like, oh, these things are actually 10-xing, 20-xing my ability to make a poker bot, but they weren't really able to make it on their own. And even Astra I feel like isn't quite able to make a really good poker bot on its own. It can make a good river solver. It can actually just on its own just a single prompt it can make an excellent river solver, but it can't really make a full-scale poker bot that well. But these models are getting a lot better at this very quickly. And I would say what's missing right now, it's kind of subtle things. It's the long-term planning and organization. They're not really good at noticing when they're going down the wrong direction and kind of stepping back and realizing, oh, actually maybe I shouldn't be doing this at all. And one thing I noticed for example is that the models if you ask them to implement CFR sometimes they will get really focused on optimizing the numerics of CFR so that it's perfect. And actually one of the important things to understand about CFR is that the numerics don't really matter that much. If there's a small amount of error, it's very robust to that. So the model spent hours and hours trying to fix the numerics for CFR when it was totally unnecessary. So it's these kinds of things that I think are missing. And these are symptoms, I think. The bigger point is that there are some things that the models are extremely good at and there are some things that they are extremely bad at. And so right now the models are complements to human ability because you can use them in all the ways that they're superhuman and then you can help fill in the gaps for all the things that they're really bad at. But over time I do expect the models to get better and better across the board. I think some things they will spike well above human performance and some things they will lag behind and some things will take longer to catch up to human performance than others. In another way, and you've said this before and lots of people have said this, that the intelligence is very jagged and so your job as a human is to understand what the model's good at and what the model's bad at, break down the problems that can be solved by the model by itself and then for the problems that they're bad at... the reason why prompting is good, a person who is good at prompting can break down problems into smaller and smaller bite-sized things that the model can solve. And so if you notice that it's jagged, okay, tell the model to do these things that it knows how to do. The things it's not as good at, break the problem down into simpler problems for the model to do well and then it can be solved. And I think that's where we're at right now, which is great. We still have some value to add. Who wants to ask another question? Okay. Yeah, well, great. We can hear you now. I was wondering, you alluded to the inherent limitations of LLMs, like you can't play Go or you can't make an amazing poker bot. Do you think that has to do with the fundamental structure of transformers? Like the order of your thoughts of like non-generative models like JEPA or world models that are also getting some attention right now? Do you think those are the solutions to the inherent limitations? I think what is it about transformers that prevents it from being good at poker or chess or go, these kinds of things? I think chain of thought, RL on chain of thought is a solution to this problem. I think that you can use these models and there are some models that are more optimized to play chess really well than others and I think if you train it to play chess really well using chain of thought, it will play chess really well. So this is a solution to this problem. It's not the only solution and it's probably not the best solution. So I do think that there are other solutions and it's definitely worth exploring the space of possible solutions. I think in many ways chain of thought is kind of a weird way to scale test time compute, right? The idea that you're going to in very interpretable English describe exactly what you're going to do. It's not... I mean you could argue that people have an inner monologue and that you could verbalize it and actually write it down, but in many ways it's often more subtle than that. So for example, sometimes people think in images and that's not exactly easy to do with chain of thought. So I think that there probably are other approaches that could be used, but I think that chain of thought is sufficient for scaling test time compute. Hey this is Jasper from Hyperbolic. Thanks for your talk. A lot of people talking about RSI and just wondering how soon do you think RSI is going to happen and can you apply it for being the best poker player in the world? Sorry, what was the last part? Do you think you can apply RSI to poker bots so that you can become the best player? Can you apply RSI to making poker bots or playing poker? They're the same, right? Like RSI basically you can self-improve yourself to become a better... I mean I asked all the latest models to make poker bots and yeah, I think they are getting pretty good at iterating and running experiments or recognizing when they could do better. As far as RSI, it is a real thing. In some ways, it's not a novel concept. You know, what's... if you think about all of human technological progress, it really is just one giant RSI loop where we use technology to advance our ability to do technology, to make technology or to make scientific discoveries. We make telescopes so we can explore deeper into space. We make computers and then we start off using punch cards and then we progress to compilers and this accelerates our ability to do research and make new technology and what humans end up doing then shifts to what the models are bad at. This goes back to my point that the models are jagged right now. So when we go from punch cards to compilers, a lot of what people end up doing becomes focused on what the models are still bad at, what the computers cannot do. And when we go from compilers to things like LLMs, again, the work that a person does shifts. Now, I think what's different today is two things. One, the generality of the technology and two, the speed at which it's happening. But fundamentally, it's not different from what's happened in the past. And even for the creation of LLMs, we have used the LLMs themselves to aid us in making the next generation of LLMs for years. I remember back in 2022, 2023, people would use LLMs to do data quality. So they would look through the pre-training data to see is this actually high quality data or is there anything concerning in it? That's basically a form of RSI. Now, the way the technology is developing, I think we're lucky that it is jagged and that there are things that the models are quite bad at. But in the same way that these models can now do superhuman math research, I do think there is a case that even if they are spiky and jagged, they can be directed towards being really good at RSI. And in that case, yeah, you probably might see very rapid progress. Now, I still think you get bottlenecked on certain things. I think you get bottlenecked by compute. I think bottlenecked by the long tail of things that the models are bad at and humans are necessary for. But I do think that things go much faster. So, we have seen a lot of rapid progress over the past few years when it comes to AI. I think even in the past six months that progress has if anything accelerated, I think. And I think that we're going to continue to see a very steep rate of progress over the next year or two. And so, perhaps my most controversial take is that if somebody is a promising student, the best thing to do is actually to drop out and join the labs now. Recruitment already. Final word. Because things are going fast and it's an exciting time, so it's not a great time to sit on the sidelines. And I will say also I've hired a few people that have just dropped out and skipped their senior year of college. And they do exceptionally well. They had a lot of success with these kinds of people. No, but I wasn't in the middle of the AI boom. All right. Well, so you want to recruit some different people here for sure. Seems like it's a talented group. Love the chat. All right. Before we let you go, a few lightning round fun questions, random questions. Is there a game in AI, a game that AI has not solved that you would like to see solved? No, I don't think there's any... I think if a team were to spend even a few months focused on... even one person actually, if one person were to spend a few months focused on making an AI to play a particular game, I think you could get the AI to play that game at a superhuman level. Palmer Luckey on War 2.0

New York Times Podcasts(576.0K)·53:21·2026-11-24
Palmer LuckeyFounder, Anduril Industries Andrew Ross SorkinHost, New York Times DealBook Summit
TL;DRAt the 2026 New York Times DealBook Summit, Anduril Industries founder Palmer Luckey argued that the U.S. must adopt a posture of unambiguous deterrence—from renaming the Pentagon to the Department of War to resuming nuclear effects testing—while warning that AI-driven cyberattacks are outpacing legacy defenses.
Throughout his conversation with Andrew Ross Sorkin, Luckey maintained that tech executives should remain "dogs on a chain" rather than dictate foreign policy, even as he defends his own deep involvement in Pentagon strategy and his recent decision to repartner with Meta's Mark...

TL;DR

At the 2026 New York Times DealBook Summit, Anduril Industries founder Palmer Luckey argued that the U.S. must adopt a posture of unambiguous deterrence—from renaming the Pentagon to the Department of War to resuming nuclear effects testing—while warning that AI-driven cyberattacks are outpacing legacy defenses. Throughout his conversation with Andrew Ross Sorkin, Luckey maintained that tech executives should remain "dogs on a chain" rather than dictate foreign policy, even as he defends his own deep involvement in Pentagon strategy and his recent decision to repartner with Meta's Mark Zuckerberg on military tech.

Cyber Warfare, AI Risks, and Civilian Preparedness

  • AI-Accelerated Cyberattacks: Luckey noted that advanced AI tools are enabling rapid exploit creation, with organizations—particularly in North Korea—leveraging the mismatch between fast-moving attackers and outdated defensive systems for financial gain (citing a concurrent PlayStation outage).
  • Uncontrollable AI Models: Addressing Pentagon scrutiny of Anthropic and OpenAI, Luckey stated empirically that AI models cannot be controlled at the use level, as "every major model has been jailbroken to some degree in a matter of months."
    • He emphasized that the real threat is "really bad, evil people with pretty good AI," rather than a sentient superintelligence, noting that AI currently lacks physical agency and data centers can still be shut down.
  • Autonomous Weapons vs. LLMs: Luckey distinguished between general-purpose LLMs (which hallucinate) and specialized military AI that processes radar, thermal imagery, and electromagnetic emanations.
    • He argued that smart, autonomous weapons are far more reliable and cause fewer civilian casualties than older analog systems or "dumb weapons," comparing public skepticism to the "self-driving car problem."
  • Basic Preparedness: Despite owning a decommissioned missile base 200 feet underground, Luckey dismissed it as an apocalypse shelter (noting a Home Depot Sawzall can cut the hinges off in 10 minutes). Instead, he advised every citizen to keep 3 days of water, 3 days of dry food, and to refill prescriptions 72 hours before running out.

The "Department of War," Deterrence, and Geopolitics

  • Renaming the Pentagon: Luckey took partial credit for pushing the Trump administration (including transition head Howard Lutnick) to rename the Department of Defense to the Department of War, citing five reasons:
    • Deterrence & Clarity: "If you want peace, prepare for war"—the name signals clear war-fighting intent rather than allowing endless, obfuscated spending on tangential issues like climate change.
    • Morale & Talent Filtering: The "War Department" won wars, whereas the "Department of Defense" lost them; the name also acts as "objector repellent" to filter out employees uncomfortable with the military's core mission.
  • China, Iran, and Stockpiles: Responding to Sorkin's citation of former MI6 chief Richard Moore—who warned Xi Jinping might miscalculate on Taiwan after watching U.S. outcomes in Iran and Ukraine—Luckey noted that China's equipment failed in Venezuela and that Xi's recent purge of his top generals buys the U.S. time.
    • Luckey defended President Trump's claim that the U.S. can fight indefinitely despite stockpile concerns, comparing it to Nixon's "madman theory" as a necessary strategic deterrent.
  • Ukraine and European Readiness: Luckey pushed back on claims that Trump is soft on Russia, arguing Trump was handed an impossible situation by European nations that ignored his first-term warnings about Russian oil—noting Germany has sent more money to Russia than Ukraine since the war began.

Corporate Power vs. Government Responsibility

  • Keeping Tech Founders on a Leash: Luckey asserted that foreign and military policy should be decided by the republic, not corporate executives, calling himself "a dog on a chain" who would go to prison if he unilaterally chose which countries to sell weapons to.
  • Defending Elon Musk on Starlink: Addressing controversies over Elon Musk limiting Starlink speeds in Ukraine, Luckey offered a "steel man" defense: if Russia views Starlink as offensive military infrastructure, it could legally justify destroying the entire global constellation, which U.S. forces also rely on.
    • Luckey argued it is untenable for the U.S. government to outsource that risk to Musk when the Pentagon itself refuses to grant Ukraine access to its own military-grade Star Shield network out of fear Russia would destroy it.
  • Project Meridian & Conflict of Interest: Sorkin read a critique from retired Air Force Master Sergeant Wes Bryant, who called Project Meridian (the Pentagon's 120-day study on warfare in 2050, co-led by Luckey and Elon Musk) indicative of "decay and oligarchy."
    • Luckey countered that over 20 people—including respected generals and admirals—sit on the MITRE-run panel, and argued the critique is partisan, noting Sorkin would never have leveled the same accusation at Eric Schmidt on the Biden-era Defense Innovation Board.

Project Meridian, Nuclear Testing, and Future Tech

  • Unchampioned Ideas for 2050: Following a Project Meridian meeting at Cheyenne Mountain, Luckey said the panel is focusing on concepts that lack existing Pentagon champions—such as weather control (e.g., altering ocean precipitation to clear views of targets)—rather than obvious trends like "killer robots."
  • Resuming Nuclear Testing: Luckey advocated for restarting nuclear weapons testing, not just to maintain the psychological credibility of Mutually Assured Destruction (MAD), but to test broadband electromagnetic pulse (EMP) effects on modern electronics.
    • Current rad-hardening guidelines are based on decades-old simulations for transistors thousands of times larger than those in modern smartphones, leaving the U.S. blind to whether an adversary's space-based nuclear air burst could disable an entire region's autonomous weapons and critical infrastructure without killing anyone.
  • Transparency in Violence: Luckey defended his support for televised public executions as a libertarian check on government power, arguing that medicalizing and hiding capital punishment trivializes it and removes public accountability—a principle he extended to real-time media coverage of war.

Nvidia, Offshore Infrastructure, and Repartnering with Meta

  • Defending Jensen Huang: Luckey explained his comment that "China wants Jensen out, so I want him in." While Luckey opposes exporting chips to China, he noted that 600,000 Nvidia H200 GPUs are currently sitting in a port because Beijing refuses import permits in order to force domestic silicon development.
    • Luckey argued that ousting Huang—which knocked $1 trillion off Nvidia's market cap—weakens America, as no interim CEO can replace Huang's personal relationships and founder-led execution.
  • Offshore Platforms: Luckey clarified that he has been working for 10 years on floating offshore platforms for regulated or "NIMBY" industries—such as metal ore processing, oil refining, desalination, and potentially AI data centers—to bypass state and local pushback.
  • Facebook Firing & Repartnering with Zuckerberg: Luckey recalled being fired from Facebook three years after selling Oculus for $2 billion in 2014—despite "greatly exceeded expectations" reviews—after donating $9,000 to a pro-Trump group in 2016.
    • While he still resents the lieutenants he made rich who "stabbed [him] in the back," he exonerated Mark Zuckerberg, noting Zuckerberg was simply acting on signals from deputies. Consequently, Luckey repartnered with Meta when Anduril took over the Army's IVAS (Infantry Vision Augmentation System) program, choosing to put soldier safety above personal grievances.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • AI Defense Advantage: "In the long run, the advantages are going to accrue to the side that is most effectively using AI for defense." — *Palmer Luckey*
  • Local AI Over Data Centers: "I actually do lean towards thinking most AI is going to run locally. I think the chips are going to get way more powerful. I think people are going to want to control their own data. I am actually somewhat skeptical of the mass scale data center as the way AI proliferates." — *Palmer Luckey*

Notable Quotes

"Every major model has been jailbroken to some degree in a matter of months. So, anyone who says that the models can be controlled isn't paying attention to what's actually going on. It's really a question of can the good guys stay ahead of the bad uses of AI." — *Palmer Luckey*
"At the end of the day, nobody should want corporate executives to have any meaningful control of our foreign policy or military policy, right? I'm a dog on a chain and I need to be kept that way." — *Palmer Luckey*
"Nobody in the West actually wants Ukraine to win. They just want them to not lose for as long as possible, which is a bizarre and absurd warfighting position." — *Palmer Luckey*
"Life and death at scale is too morally fraught an area to not bring your absolute best tool to the table, even if it makes people feel icky... I don't think there's any right number of people to die for not feeling yucky." — *Palmer Luckey*

So like I have this working theory in my mind that mutually assured destruction relies on a certain level of psychic belief in the power of nuclear weapons at a population level. We know that MAD works as a deterrent strategy when everyone is afraid of nuclear weapons. We do not have any evidence that it works when people are not. And when you show that you're not even willing to test things, I think that that robs mutually assured destruction as a concept of a lot of credibility. I have to admit I'm not a philosopher, nor am I a tactician, nor am I a strategist. You know, there's actually a much more boring reason that I want to restart nuclear weapons testing. We need to test not the weapon itself, but the impact of nuclear weapons on the technology that underpins our military and our society, lest we accidentally make use of nuclear weapons an impossibly irresistible use of force. So, like I'll give you an example. In the '60s, the '70s, the '80s, the early '90s, we weren't just blowing up nukes to watch what they did. In almost every test, we were lining up rows of civilian pickup trucks, military radios, washing machines, respirators, ventilators, everything you need to run hospitals, power plants, substations, oil refineries. And we were watching not just what the blast and temperature effects had on them, but also the electronic effects. What is an electromagnetic pulse? Broadband electromagnetic pulse, not something like a directed-energy microwave weapon, but a broadband massive pulse. What does that do? Which things survive? Which shut down, but that are able to be restarted? Which of them are completely fried and will never operate again? We learned a lot from that. And we spent a lot of money hardening military infrastructure and critical infrastructure and power and hospital infrastructure. There's a lot of stuff in hospitals.
